Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given an array consisting of $n$ integers $a_1$, $a_2$, ..., $a_n$. Initially $a_x = 1$, all other elements are equal to $0$.

You have to perform $m$ operations. During the $i$-th operation, you choose two indices $c$ and $d$ such that $l_i \le c, d \le r_i$, and swap $a_c$ and $a_d$.

Calculate the number of indices $k$ such that it is possible to choose the operations so that $a_k = 1$ in the end.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 100$) — the number of test cases. Then the description of $t$ testcases follow.

The first line of each test case contains three integers $n$, $x$ and $m$ ($1 \le n \le 10^9$; $1 \le m \le 100$; $1 \le x \le n$).

Each of next $m$ lines contains the descriptions of the operations; the $i$-th line contains two integers $l_i$ and $r_i$ ($1 \le l_i \le r_i \le n$).


-----Output-----

For each test case print one integer — the number of indices $k$ such that it is possible to choose the operations so that $a_k = 1$ in the end.


-----Example-----
Input
3
6 4 3
1 6
2 3
5 5
4 1 2
2 4
1 2
3 3 2
2 3
1 2

Output
6
2
3



-----Note-----

In the first test case, it is possible to achieve $a_k = 1$ for every $k$. To do so, you may use the following operations:  swap $a_k$ and $a_4$;  swap $a_2$ and $a_2$;  swap $a_5$ and $a_5$. 

In the second test case, only $k = 1$ and $k = 2$ are possible answers. To achieve $a_1 = 1$, you have to swap $a_1$ and $a_1$ during the second operation. To achieve $a_2 = 1$, you have to swap $a_1$ and $a_2$ during the second operation.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Algorithm:

```python
def solve():
    n, x, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Start with reachable set as a list of intervals
    intervals = [(x, x)]
    
    for _ in range(m):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        
        # Check if any interval in intervals overlaps with [l, r]
        has_overlap = False
        for interval in intervals:
            a, b = interval
            if max(a, l) <= min(b, r):  # overlap exists
                has_overlap = True
                break
                
        if has_overlap:
            # We can now have the 1 anywhere in [l, r]
            # Add the interval [l, r] to our intervals and merge
            intervals.append((l, r))
            
            # Now merge overlapping or adjacent intervals
            # Sort by left endpoint
            intervals.sort()
            
            # Merge intervals
            merged = []
            for interval in intervals:
                if not merged:
                    merged.append(interval)
                else:
                    last = merged[-1]
                    if interval[0] <= last[1] + 1:  # overlapping or adjacent
                        # Merge: extend the last interval
                        merged[-1] = (last[0], max(last[1], interval[1]))
                    else:
                        merged.append(interval)
            
            intervals = merged
    
    # Calculate total length
    total_length = 0
    for l, r in intervals:
        total_length += r - l + 1
    
    return total_length
```

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, x, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    intervals = [(x, x)]
    
    for _ in range(m):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        
        # Check if any interval overlaps with [l, r]
        has_overlap = False
        for a, b in intervals:
            if max(a, l) <= min(b, r):
                has_overlap = True
                break
                
        if has_overlap:
            intervals.append((l, r))
            # Sort intervals by left endpoint
            intervals.sort()
            # Merge overlapping or adjacent intervals
            merged = []
            for interval in intervals:
                if not merged:
                    merged.append(interval)
                else:
                    last = merged[-1]
                    if interval[0] <= last[1] + 1:
                        merged[-1] = (last[0], max(last[1], interval[1]))
                    else:
                        merged.append(interval)
            intervals = merged
    
    total_length = sum(r - l + 1 for l, r in intervals)
    print(total_length)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We start with an array of n integers where only `a[x] = 1` and all others are 0.
2. We have m operations, each operation allows us to swap two elements within a given range [l_i, r_i].
3. We need to find how many indices k can have the value 1 after all operations.

## Key Insights

1. The 1 starts at position x.
2. Through swaps within ranges, the 1 can move to other positions.
3. The 1 can only move to positions that are "connected" through the operations - meaning we can form a path of overlapping ranges that allow the 1 to propagate.

4. The key insight is that if we can connect position x to position k through a sequence of overlapping ranges, then k can potentially have the 1.

5. Essentially, we need to find the connected component that contains position x after considering all the ranges.

6. However, since we can perform swaps within each range, if two ranges overlap, we can transfer the 1 between them.

7. The algorithm: we need to find all positions that are reachable from x through the given ranges.

## Algorithm

Instead of trying to simulate the swaps (which would be computationally expensive due to large n), we can think about connectivity:

1. The 1 starts at position x.
2. For each operation [l, r], if the 1 is currently in the range [l, r], we can swap it with any other position in [l, r], meaning the 1 can now be at any position within [l, r] (by swapping with appropriate elements).

3. So, the reachable positions form a connected component. We can use a union-find or simply track the current intervals.

4. Since m ≤ 100, we can simulate the merging of intervals step by step.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Start with the initial position x as the only reachable position.
2. For each operation [l, r], if the current reachable interval overlaps with [l, r], we can merge them.
3. After processing all operations, the final reachable interval will contain all positions that can have the 1.

Wait, let me reconsider. The key insight is simpler:

- Initially, the 1 is at position x.
- After each operation, if the current position of 1 (or any position in the connected component) falls within [l, r], we can move the 1 to any position in [l, r].
- So we need to maintain the current set of positions where the 1 can be.

But since n can be very large (up to 10^9), we can't store all positions. Instead, we use interval representation.

Actually, the simpler approach: maintain a set of intervals that represent all positions where the 1 can currently be. Initially, it's just [x, x].

For each operation [l, r]:
- If any part of our current intervals overlaps with [l, r], we merge all intervals that overlap with [l, r] into a larger interval.

The final answer is the length of the union of all intervals after processing all operations.

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1**: n=6, x=4, m=3, operations: [1,6], [2,3], [5,5]
- Start: interval [4,4]
- Op [1,6]: [1,6] overlaps with [4,4] → merge to [1,6]
- Op [2,3]: [2,3] overlaps with [1,6] → already fully covered
- Op [5,5]: [5,5] overlaps with [1,6] → already covered
- Final interval: [1,6], length = 6 ✓

**Example 2**: n=4, x=1, m=2, operations: [2,4], [1,2]
- Start: interval [1,1]
- Op [2,4]: [2,4] doesn't overlap with [1,1]? Wait, 1 is not in [2,4] → no overlap
- So after this op, we still have [1,1]
- Op [1,2]: [1,2] overlaps with [1,1] → merge to [1,2]
- Final interval: [1,2], length = 2 ✓

**Example 3**: n=3, x=3, m=2, operations: [2,3], [1,2]
- Start: interval [3,3]
- Op [2,3]: [2,3] overlaps with [3,3] → merge to [2,3]
- Op [1,2]: [1,2] overlaps with [2,3] → merge to [1,3]
- Final interval: [1,3], length = 3 ✓

This matches the expected output.

## Implementation

I'll implement the interval merging approach:

1. Start with initial interval [x, x]
2. For each operation [l, r]:
   - Create new intervals by merging current intervals that overlap with [l, r]
   - Update the current intervals
3. Return the total length of the merged intervals

```python
def solve():
    n, x, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Start with initial position x
    intervals = [[x, x]]
    
    for _ in range(m):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        
        # Find all intervals that overlap with [l, r]
        new_intervals = []
        merged = False
        
        for interval in intervals:
            start, end = interval
            # Check if current interval overlaps with [l, r]
            if start <= r and end >= l:  # overlapping condition
                # Merge the current interval with [l, r]
                new_start = min(start, l)
                new_end = max(end, r)
                new_intervals.append([new_start, new_end])
                merged = True
            else:
                # No overlap, keep the current interval
                new_intervals.append(interval)
        
        intervals = new_intervals
    
    # Calculate total length of all intervals
    total_length = 0
    intervals.sort()
    
    for start, end in intervals:
        total_length += end - start + 1
    
    return total_length

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

Wait, I need to be more careful about the merging. When an interval overlaps with [l,r], we should merge them, but we should only merge with the overlapping part. Actually, my current approach might miss some cases.

Let me reconsider: if we have an interval [a,b] and we have an operation [l,r], then if a ≤ r and b ≥ l, they overlap, and we should merge them to [min(a,l), max(b,r)].

But my current logic above does this correctly: new_start = min(start, l), new_end = max(end, r).

Also, when we process the operation, we merge all intervals that overlap with [l,r], regardless of whether they were already merged with previous operations. This should be correct.

Let me verify with example 2 again:
- Start: [[1,1]]
- Op [2,4]: [2,4] doesn't overlap with [1,1] (since 1 > 4? no, 1 ≤ 4 but 1 < 2, so 1 > 4 is false, but condition is start <= r and end >= l → 1 <= 4 and 1 >= 2? 1 >= 2 is false → no overlap)
- So no merge, intervals remain [[1,1]]
- Op [1,2]: [1,2] overlaps with [1,1]? 1 <= 2 and 1 >= 1 → true → merge to [min(1,1), max(1,2)] = [1,2]
- Final length = 2 ✓

Example 3 again:
- Start: [[3,3]]
- Op [2,3]: 3 <= 3 and 3 >= 2 → true → merge to [min(3,2), max(3,3)] = [2,3]
- Op [1,2]: 2 <= 2 and 3 >= 1 → true → merge to [min(2,1), max(3,2)] = [1,3]
- Final length = 3 ✓

It looks correct.

But what if we have multiple overlapping intervals? For example, if we have intervals [[1,3], [5,6]] and an operation [3,4]?
- [1,3] overlaps with [3,4]: 1 <= 4 and 3 >= 3 → true → merge to [min(1,3), max(3,4)] = [1,4]
- [5,6] doesn't overlap with [3,4]: 5 <= 4? false → no merge
- So we get [[1,4], [5,6]]

This seems correct.

```python
def solve():
    n, x, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Start with initial position x
    intervals = [[x, x]]
    
    for _ in range(m):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        
        new_intervals = []
        for interval in intervals:
            start, end = interval
            # Check if current interval [start, end] overlaps with [l, r]
            # Overlap condition: start <= r AND end >= l
            if start <= r and end >= l:
                # Merge them: new interval is [min(start,l), max(end,r)]
                new_start = min(start, l)
                new_end = max(end, r)
                new_intervals.append([new_start, new_end])
            else:
                # No overlap, keep the original interval
                new_intervals.append(interval)
        
        intervals = new_intervals
    
    # Calculate total length of all intervals
    total_length = 0
    for start, end in intervals:
        total_length += end - start + 1
    
    return total_length

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

                new_start = min(start, l)

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

You are given an array consisting of $n$ integers $a_1$, $a_2$, ..., $a_n$. Initially $a_x = 1$, all other elements are equal to $0$.

You have to perform $m$ operations. During the $i$-th operation, you choose two indices $c$ and $d$ such that $l_i \le c, d \le r_i$, and swap $a_c$ and $a_d$.

Calculate the number of indices $k$ such that it is possible to choose the operations so that $a_k = 1$ in the end.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 100$) — the number of test cases. Then the description of $t$ testcases follow.

The first line of each test case contains three integers $n$, $x$ and $m$ ($1 \le n \le 10^9$; $1 \le m \le 100$; $1 \le x \le n$).

Each of next $m$ lines contains the descriptions of the operations; the $i$-th line contains two integers $l_i$ and $r_i$ ($1 \le l_i \le r_i \le n$).


-----Output-----

For each test case print one integer — the number of indices $k$ such that it is possible to choose the operations so that $a_k = 1$ in the end.


-----Example-----
Input
3
6 4 3
1 6
2 3
5 5
4 1 2
2 4
1 2
3 3 2
2 3
1 2

Output
6
2
3



-----Note-----

In the first test case, it is possible to achieve $a_k = 1$ for every $k$. To do so, you may use the following operations:  swap $a_k$ and $a_4$;  swap $a_2$ and $a_2$;  swap $a_5$ and $a_5$. 

In the second test case, only $k = 1$ and $k = 2$ are possible answers. To achieve $a_1 = 1$, you have to swap $a_1$ and $a_1$ during the second operation. I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of n integers, initially only `a[x] = 1`, all others are 0.
2. We perform m operations, each operation allows us to swap two elements within a given range [l_i, r_i].
3. We need to find how many indices k can end up with value 1 after all operations.

## Key Insight

The key insight is that the 1 can move through the array only if there's a sequence of operations that can "connect" the initial position x to the target position k.

Each operation [l_i, r_i] allows us to swap any two elements within that range. This means that if the 1 is in a position within [l_i, r_i], we can potentially move it to any other position within that same range.

So the question becomes: which positions can the 1 reach through a series of such operations?

## Approach

We can think of this as a connectivity problem:
- Start with position x
- For each operation [l_i, r_i], if the current range of possible positions overlaps with [l_i, r_i], then we can extend our range of possible positions to include the entire interval [min(current_left, l_i), max(current_right, r_i)]
- The final answer is the size of the connected interval we can reach

However, since we have only m ≤ 100 operations, we can simulate the process:

1. Start with the initial position x
2. For each operation [l_i, r_i], if the current interval [left, right] intersects with [l_i, r_i], we can expand our reachable interval
3. The final reachable interval will be [min_left, max_right] and the answer is (max_right - min_left + 1)

## Algorithm Steps

1. Initialize the current reachable range as just [x, x]
2. For each operation [l_i, r_i]:
   - If the current range overlaps with [l_i, r_i], expand the range to cover the union of the two intervals
   - If they don't overlap, no change
3. Return the length of the final range

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n, x, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Start with the initial position x
    left = x
    right = x
    
    for _ in range(m):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        
        # Check if current range [left, right] intersects with [l, r]
        # Two intervals intersect if left <= r and l <= right
        if left <= r and l <= right:
            # They intersect, so we can expand our range
            new_left = min(left, l)
            new_right = max(right, r)
            left = new_left
            right = new_right
    
    # The number of indices that can have 1 in the end
    return right - left + 1

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

## Verification with Examples

**Example 1**: n=6, x=4, m=3, operations: [1,6], [2,3], [5,5]
- Start: [4,4]
- Operation [1,6]: 4 ≤ 6 and 1 ≤ 4 → intersect, new range = [min(4,1), max(4,6)] = [1,6]
- Operation [2,3]: 1 ≤ 3 and 2 ≤ 6 → intersect, new range = [min(1,2), max(6,3)] = [1,6]
- Operation [5,5]: 1 ≤ 5 and 5 ≤ 6 → intersect, new range = [min(1,5), max(6,5)] = [1,6]
- Answer: 6-1+1 = 6 ✓

**Example 2**: n=4, x=1, m=2, operations: [2,4], [1,2]
- Start: [1,1]
- Operation [2,4]: 1 ≤ 4 and 2 ≤ 1 → false (2 > 1), no intersection → remains [1,1]
- Operation [1,2]: 1 ≤ 2 and 1 ≤ 1 → true, new range = [min(1,1), max(1,2)] = [1,2]
- Answer: 2-1+1 = 2 ✓

**Example 3**: n=3, x=3, m=2, operations: [2,3], [1,2]
- Start: [3,3]
- Operation [2,3]: 3 ≤ 3 and 2 ≤ 3 → true, new range = [min(3,2), max(3,3)] = [2,3]
- Operation [1,2]: 2 ≤ 2 and 1 ≤ 3 → true, new range = [min(2,1), max(3,2)] = [1,3]
- Answer: 3-1+1 = 3 ✓

The solution works correctly for all test cases.

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We start with an array of n integers where only `a[x] = 1` and all others are 0.
2. We have m operations, each with a range `[l_i, r_i]`.
3. In each operation, we can swap any two elements within the given range.
4. We need to find how many indices k can have `a[k] = 1` after all operations.

## Key Insight

The crucial observation is that if we can move the 1 from position x to any other position k, then k is a valid answer.

When we have an operation `[l, r]`, we can swap any two elements in that range. This means that if the current position of the 1 is within `[l, r]`, we can potentially move it to any other position within that range.

However, the movement is constrained by the sequence of operations. We need to track the connected components of positions that the 1 can reach through a series of operations.

## Approach

The key insight is that the 1 can move from one position to another if there's a chain of operations where each operation connects the current position to a broader range, eventually allowing the 1 to reach any position within the "union" of all reachable ranges.

But there's a simpler way to think about it: the 1 can reach a position k if and only if k is in the same connected component as x, where connectivity is defined by operations.

More specifically, we can think of it as:
- Initially, the 1 is at position x.
- For each operation `[l, r]`, if the current position of the 1 is in `[l, r]`, then we can move it to any position in `[l, r]` (by swapping it with the desired position).
- So the reachable positions form a union of intervals that get extended as we process operations.

However, since we can perform operations in any order (we just need to see if it's possible), we can consider the final reachability.

Actually, the simpler approach is:
1. Start with the initial position x.
2. For each operation, if the current reachable range overlaps with `[l, r]`, we can extend our reach to the entire interval `[l, r]`.
3. The final reachable positions are the union of all intervals that are connected through operations.

But we don't need to simulate every possible sequence - we just need to find the final connected component.

## Algorithm

1. Start with a set of reachable positions that initially just contains position x.
2. For each operation `[l, r]`:
   - If any position in our current reachable set falls within `[l, r]`, then we can extend our reachable set to cover the entire interval `[l, r]`.
3. The answer is the size of the final reachable set.

But wait - since the array is huge (up to 10^9), we can't store all positions. We need to work with intervals.

Actually, we can represent the reachable positions as a set of intervals. Initially, we have one interval containing just x.

For each operation `[l, r]`:
- Check if our current intervals overlap with `[l, r]`.
- If they overlap, merge the intervals to form a larger connected region.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Initialize the reachable region as a list of intervals, starting with `[(x, x)]`.
2. For each operation `[l, r]`:
   - Check if any interval in our current set overlaps with `[l, r]`.
   - If there's overlap, merge the intervals to form a new set that includes the entire `[l, r]` range.
3. Count the total number of positions in all merged intervals.

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1**: n=6, x=4, m=3, operations: [1,6], [2,3], [5,5]
- Start: [(4,4)]
- Operation [1,6]: 4 is in [1,6], so merge → [(1,6)]
- Operation [2,3]: 2,3 are in [1,6], so already covered (no change)
- Operation [5,5]: 5 is in [1,6], so already covered
- Final: positions 1 to 6 → 6 positions ✓

**Example 2**: n=4, x=1, m=2, operations: [2,4], [1,2]
- Start: [(1,1)]
- Operation [2,4]: 1 is not in [2,4], so no overlap → still [(1,1)]
- Operation [1,2]: 1 is in [1,2], so we can extend to [1,2] 
- After merging: [(1,2)]
- Final: positions 1,2 → 2 positions ✓

**Example 3**: n=3, x=3, m=2, operations: [2,3], [1,2]
- Start: [(3,3)]
- Operation [2,3]: 3 is in [2,3], so merge → [(2,3)]
- Operation [1,2]: 2 is in [1,2] and 2 is in [2,3], so we can extend to [1,2] 
- After merging: [(1,3)] 
- Final: positions 1,2,3 → 3 positions ✓

Wait, in example 3: after first op [2,3], we have position 3, then we can move it to position 2 (since 2,3 are in range), so we have 2,3. Then with second op [1,2], since position 2 is in [1,2], we can move to position 1. So we get 1,2,3.

Yes, correct.

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n, x, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Start with initial position x
    intervals = [(x, x)]
    
    for _ in range(m):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        
        # Check if any interval overlaps with [l, r]
        new_intervals = []
        overlap_found = False
        
        for start, end in intervals:
            # Check if current interval overlaps with [l, r]
            if start <= r and end >= l:  # overlap exists
                overlap_found = True
                
                # Merge the current interval with [l, r]
                new_start = min(start, l)
                new_end = max(end, r)
                # We'll merge this new interval later
                new_intervals.append((new_start, new_end))
            else:
                # No overlap, keep the original interval
                new_intervals.append((start, end))
        
        # If we found overlap, we need to merge all overlapping intervals
        if overlap_found:
            # Sort intervals by start to merge properly
            new_intervals.sort()
            merged = []
            for start, end in new_intervals:
                if not merged:
                    merged.append((start, end))
                else:
                    last_start, last_end = merged[-1]
                    if start <= last_end:
                        # Overlap or adjacent, merge
                        merged[-1] = (last_start, max(last_end, end))
                    else:
                        merged.append((start, end))
            intervals = merged
        else:
            # No overlap, keep original intervals
            intervals = new_intervals
    
    # Calculate total number of positions
    total = 0
    for start, end in intervals:
        total += end - start + 1
    
    return total

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

```python
def solve():
    n, x, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Start with initial position x
    intervals = [(x, x)]
    
    for _ in range(m):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        
        # Create new intervals for this operation
        new_intervals = []
        overlap_found = False
        
        for start, end in intervals:
            # Check if current interval overlaps with [l, r]
            if start <= r and end >= l:  # overlap exists
                overlap_found = True
                # Merge the current interval with [l, r]
                new_start = min(start, l)
                new_end = max(end, r)
                new_intervals.append((new_start, new_end))
            else:
                # No overlap, keep the original interval
                new_intervals.append((start, end))
        
        # If we found overlap, merge all intervals in new_intervals
        if overlap_found:
            # Sort by start to merge properly
            new_intervals.sort()
            merged = []
            for start, end in new_intervals:
                if not merged:
                    merged.append((start, end))
                else:
                    last_start, last_end = merged[-1]
                    if start <= last_end:
                        # Overlap or adjacent, merge
                        merged[-1] = (last_start, max(last_end, end))
                    else:
                        merged.append((start, end))
            intervals = merged
        else:
            # No overlap found, just keep the new_intervals
            intervals = new_intervals
    
    # Calculate total number of positions
    total = 0
    for start, end in intervals:
        total += end - start + 1
    
    return total

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
def solve():
    n, x, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Start with initial position x
    intervals = [(x, x)]
    
    for _ in range(m):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        
        # Create new intervals for this operation
        new_intervals = []
        overlap_found = False
        
        for start, end in intervals:
            # Check if current interval overlaps with [l, r]
            if start <= r and end >= l:  # overlap exists
                overlap_found = True
                # Merge the current interval with [l, r]
                new_start = min(start, l)
                new_end = max(end, r)
                new_intervals.append((new_start, new_end))
            else:
                # No overlap, keep the original interval
                new_intervals.append((start, end))
        
        # If we found overlap, merge all intervals in new_intervals
        if overlap_found:
            # Sort by start to merge properly
            new_intervals.sort()
            merged = []
            for start, end in new_intervals:
                if not merged:
                    merged.append((start, end))
                else:
                    last_start, last_end = merged[-1]
                    if start <= last_end:
                        # Overlap or adjacent, merge
                        merged[-1] = (last_start, max(last_end, end))
                    else:
                        merged.append((start, end))
            intervals = merged
        else:
            # No overlap found, just keep the new_intervals
            intervals = new_intervals
    
    # Calculate total number of positions
    total = 0
    for start, end in intervals:
        total += end - start + 1
    
    return total

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())

1. Initialize left = x, right = x

```python
for _ in range(t):
    n, x, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    left = x

        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        # Check if current interval [left, right] overlaps with [l, r]
        if left <= r and l <= right:
            left = min(left, l)
            right = max(right, r)
    
    print(right - left + 1)
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of n integers, initially only `a_x = 1` and all others are 0.
2. We perform m operations, each operation allows us to swap two elements within a given range [l_i, r_i].
3. We need to find how many indices k can potentially have the value 1 after all operations.

## Key Insights

1. The value 1 starts at position x.
2. Through swaps, the 1 can move from its current position to other positions.
3. The question is: which positions can the 1 reach after all possible operations?

## Critical Observations

1. If we can connect position x to position k through a sequence of operations, then k is reachable.
2. Each operation [l_i, r_i] allows us to swap any two positions within that range.
3. This means that if two positions are in the same connected component across all operations, we can potentially swap values between them.

Actually, let me think more carefully about what happens with swaps:

- If we have a range [l, r], we can swap any two positions within [l, r].
- This means that all positions in the range [l, r] become connected - we can move any value from one position to another within the range through a series of swaps.
- The key insight is that positions that are in the same "connected component" across all operations can exchange values.

But here's the crucial point: we start with 1 at position x. After all operations, the 1 can be at any position k that is in the same connected component as x.

Wait, let me reconsider: actually, with swaps, we can move the 1 through any sequence of operations. So if the 1 can reach position k through a chain of operations, then k is reachable.

The connected component approach is correct: two positions are in the same component if there's a sequence of ranges that connect them.

But since we have m operations (and m ≤ 100), we can compute the connected component of x using union-find or simply by simulating the connectivity.

However, n can be up to 10^9, so we can't create an array of size n. We need to work with ranges.

## Algorithm

1. We start with x in a "connected component".
2. For each operation [l_i, r_i], if this range overlaps with our current component, we extend our component to include all positions in that range.
3. More precisely, we maintain the current interval (or set of intervals) that contains x.
4. For each operation, if there's any overlap between the operation range and our current component, we merge the two ranges.

But we need to be careful: the operations are processed sequentially, and each operation allows swapping within its range.

The key insight is that after processing all operations, the 1 can reach any position that is in the union of all ranges that are "connected" to the initial position x through a chain of overlapping ranges.

Actually, the most direct approach:
- Start with the initial position x.
- For each operation range [l_i, r_i], if this range overlaps with our current reachable positions, then we can extend our reachable positions to include all positions in [l_i, r_i].
- We can maintain the current interval of reachable positions as a set of intervals.

Since m ≤ 100, we can simulate this efficiently.

## Step-by-step solution

1. Start with a set of intervals containing only x (i.e., interval [x, x]).
2. For each operation [l, r]:
   - Find the intersection between each current interval and [l, r].
   - If there's any intersection, merge the intervals.
3. After processing all operations, the answer is the total length of all resulting intervals.

Let me trace through the first example:
- n=6, x=4, m=3
- Operations: [1,6], [2,3], [5,5]

Operation 2: [2,3] - overlaps with [1,6], so no change (already contained)
Operation 3: [5,5] - overlaps with [1,6], so no change

- n=4, x=1, m=2
- Operations: [2,4], [1,2]

- Operations: [2,3], [1,2]

## Implementation

I'll implement the interval merging algorithm:

```python
def solve():
    n, x, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Start with initial position x as a single interval
    intervals = [[x, x]]
    
    for _ in range(m):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        
        # Find new intervals to merge with current intervals
        new_intervals = []
        
        for interval in intervals:
            left, right = interval
            # Check if current interval overlaps with [l, r]
            if left <= r and right >= l:
                # They overlap, merge them
                new_left = min(left, l)
                new_right = max(right, r)
                new_intervals.append([new_left, new_right])
            else:
                # No overlap, keep the current interval
                new_intervals.append(interval)
        
        # Update intervals to new ones
        intervals = new_intervals
    
    # Calculate total length of all intervals
    total_length = 0
    for left, right in intervals:
        total_length += right - left + 1
    
    return total_length

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

Wait, let me double-check the overlap condition:
Two intervals [a,b] and [c,d] overlap if and only if a <= d and c <= b.

In my code above, I'm checking if left <= r and right >= l, which is equivalent to: 
- left <= r (interval starts before or at r)
- right >= l (interval ends at or after l)

This means they overlap.

Actually, the intervals overlap if and only if the intersection is non-empty, which happens when max(left, l) <= min(right, r).

But my condition left <= r and right >= l is equivalent to max(left, l) <= min(right, r)? Let me verify:

If left <= r and right >= l, then:
- max(left, l) <= min(right, r)
- Because if left <= r and right >= l, then max(left, l) ≤ max(right, r) and min(left, r) ≤ min(right, l)... wait, let me check with examples.

Actually, the condition for overlap is: left <= r and l <= right.

In my code, I have "left <= r and right >= l" which is the same as "left <= r and l <= right". ✓

So the condition is correct.

Example 1: intervals start as [[4,4]]
- Operation [1,6]: 4 <= 6 and 1 <= 4 → true, so merge to [min(4,1), max(4,6)] = [1,6]
- Operation [2,3]: 1 <= 3 and 2 <= 6 → true, so merge to [min(1,2), max(6,3)] = [1,6]
- Operation [5,5]: 1 <= 5 and 5 <= 6 → true, so merge to [min(1,5), max(6,5)] = [1,6]
Result: length 6 ✓

- Operation [2,4]: 1 <= 4 and 2 <= 1? 2 <= 1 is false → no overlap → keep [1,1]

Example 3: start [[3,3]]

You have to perform $m$ operations. During the $i$-th operation, you choose two indices $c$ and $d$ such that $l_i \le c, d \le r_i$, and swap $a_c$ and $a_d$.

Calculate the number of indices $k$ such that it is possible to choose the operations so that $a_k = 1$ in the end.


-----Input-----

The first line contains a single integer $t$ ($1 \le t \le 100$) — the number of test cases. Then the description of $t$ testcases follow.

The first line of each test case contains three integers $n$, $x$ and $m$ ($1 \le n \le 10^9$; $1 \le m \le 100$; $1 \le x \le n$).

Each of next $m$ lines contains the descriptions of the operations; the $i$-th line contains two integers $l_i$ and $r_i$ ($1 \le l_i \le r_i \le n$).


-----Output-----

For each test case print one integer — the number of indices $k$ such that it is possible to choose the operations so that $a_k = 1$ in the end.


-----Example-----
Input
3

1 6

4 1 2

1 2

2 3
1 2

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of n integers, initially with `a_x = 1` and all others = 0
2. We perform m operations, each operation allows us to swap two elements within a given range [l_i, r_i]
3. We need to find how many indices k can have the value 1 at the end

## Key Insights

1. The value 1 starts at position x
2. Through swaps, we can potentially move the 1 to other positions
3. The 1 can move from one position to another only if there's a sequence of operations that connects the positions
4. Two positions are "connected" if we can transfer the 1 from one to the other through a chain of valid swaps

## Critical Observations

1. When we have a swap operation [l, r], it means we can swap any two elements in the range [l, r]
2. This means that within any range that is covered by at least one operation, elements can be freely permuted among those positions
3. However, the key insight is that if we have a range [l, r] where we can perform swaps, then any element within that range can potentially be moved to any other position within that range

4. The 1 initially starts at position x. After each operation, if x is within the range [l_i, r_i], we can potentially move the 1 to any position within that range.

5. Therefore, we need to find the set of positions that are reachable from x through a sequence of operations.

6. This is equivalent to finding the union of all intervals that contain x or can be connected through overlapping ranges.

## Algorithm

1. Start with the initial position x
2. Keep track of all positions that can have the 1
3. For each operation [l, r]:
   - If the current reachable range overlaps with [l, r], we can expand our reachable range
4. The key is to maintain the connected component of positions that can contain the 1

Wait, let me reconsider. Since we can swap any two elements within a range, if the initial position x is in a range [l, r], then after that operation, the 1 can be moved to any position in that range.

So the reachable positions form a continuous interval (or union of intervals) that starts from x and can expand outward through overlapping operations.

Actually, let's think about it more systematically:

- Initially, position x can have the 1
- After operation [l, r], if x is in [l, r], then we can move the 1 to any position in [l, r]
- So we expand our reachable set to include all positions in [l, r]

But what if x is not in [l, r]? Then we can't move the 1 into this range.

The key insight is that the 1 can only be moved through operations that include its current position.

So we start with a set of positions that can contain the 1: initially just {x}

For each operation [l, r]:
- Let current_reachable = current set of positions that can have 1
- For each position p in current_reachable, if p is in [l, r], then we can move the 1 to any position in [l, r]
- So we add all positions in [l, r] that are not already in our reachable set

But wait - since we can swap any two positions in [l, r], if we have the 1 at any position p in [l, r], we can move it to any position in [l, r].

So if we have the 1 at position p and p is in [l, r], then after the operation, we can have the 1 at any position in [l, r].

Therefore, the reachable positions form a continuous interval that grows as we process operations.

We can maintain the current interval of positions that can have the 1. Initially, it's [x, x].

For each operation [l, r]:
- If the current interval overlaps with [l, r], then we merge them
- The new interval will be the union of the current interval and [l, r] (taking the intersection of all overlapping ranges)

Actually, it's simpler: if our current reachable set has any position in [l, r], then we can expand our reachable set to include all of [l, r].

So if the current reachable interval [left, right] overlaps with [l, r], then we can extend it to cover [min(left, l), max(right, r)].

But initially we have just position x.

So the algorithm:
1. current_left = x, current_right = x
2. For each operation [l, r]:
   - If l <= current_right and current_left <= r (i.e., if [l, r] overlaps with [current_left, current_right]):
     - current_left = min(current_left, l)
     - current_right = max(current_right, r)
3. Answer = current_right - current_left + 1

Wait, let me verify with the examples:

**Example 1:** n=6, x=4, m=3, operations: [1,6], [2,3], [5,5]
- Start: current_left=4, current_right=4
- Operation [1,6]: 1 <= 4 and 4 <= 6 → overlap, so current_left=min(4,1)=1, current_right=max(4,6)=6
- Operation [2,3]: 2 <= 6 and 6 <= 3? No, 6 <= 3 is false. Also need to check overlap: current_left=1, current_right=6, so 2 <= 6 and 1 <= 3 → yes, overlap. So current_left=min(1,2)=1, current_right=max(6,3)=6
- Operation [5,5]: 5 <= 6 and 5 <= 5 → yes, overlap. So current_left=min(1,5)=1, current_right=max(6,5)=6
- Answer = 6-1+1 = 6 ✓

**Example 2:** n=4, x=1, m=2, operations: [2,4], [1,2]
- Start: current_left=1, current_right=1  
- Operation [2,4]: 2 <= 1? No, 2 > 1, so no overlap? Wait, we need l <= current_right AND current_left <= r
  - l=2, r=4; current_left=1, current_right=1
  - Is 2 <= 1? No, so no overlap
- Operation [1,2]: l=1, r=2; current_left=1, current_right=1
  - Is 1 <= 1 and 1 <= 2? Yes, so overlap
  - current_left = min(1,1)=1, current_right = max(1,2)=2
- Answer = 2-1+1 = 2 ✓

**Example 3:** n=3, x=3, m=2, operations: [2,3], [1,2]
- Start: current_left=3, current_right=3
- Operation [2,3]: l=2, r=3; current_left=3, current_right=3
  - Is 2 <= 3 and 3 <= 3? Yes → overlap
  - current_left = min(3,2)=2, current_right = max(3,3)=3
- Operation [1,2]: l=1, r=2; current_left=2, current_right=3
  - Is 1 <= 3 and 2 <= 2? Yes → overlap
  - current_left = min(2,1)=1, current_right = max(3,2)=3
- Answer = 3-1+1 = 3 ✓

Perfect! This matches all examples.

## Final Algorithm

For each test case:
1. Read n, x, m
2. Initialize current_left = x, current_right = x
3. For each operation [l, r]:
   - If l <= current_right and current_left <= r (overlap condition):
     - current_left = min(current_left, l)
     - current_right = max(current_right, r)
4. Answer = current_right - current_left + 1

```python
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n, x, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Initialize the current range of positions where 1 can be
    current_left = x
    current_right = x
    
    # Process each operation
    for _ in range(m):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        # Check if the current range [current_left, current_right] overlaps with [l, r]
        if l <= current_right and current_left <= r:
            # There's overlap, so we can extend our range
            current_left = min(current_left, l)
            current_right = max(current_right, r)
    
    # The number of indices where we can have 1 at the end
    result = current_right - current_left + 1
    print(result)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have an array of n integers, initially with `a[x] = 1` and all others = 0
2. We perform m operations, each operation allows us to swap elements at positions c and d where `l_i ≤ c, d ≤ r_i`
3. We need to find how many indices k can end up with value 1 after all operations

## Key Insight

The question is about connectivity - if we can move the 1 from position x to other positions through a series of swaps, then those positions can have 1 in the end.

Each operation defines a range [l_i, r_i] where we can swap any two elements within that range. This means that within each operation's range, elements can be freely rearranged among themselves (since we can perform multiple swaps).

So the key insight is: **the 1 can reach any position that lies within the "connected component" of the initial position x, where connectivity is defined by overlapping ranges.**

More precisely, starting from position x, we can move the 1 to any position that is reachable through a sequence of operations where each operation connects some range that overlaps with the current range of the 1.

## Algorithm

1. Start with the initial position x
2. Keep track of all positions that can potentially contain the 1
3. For each operation, if the current reachable range overlaps with [l_i, r_i], we can expand our reachable range to include the entire interval [l_i, r_i]
4. We need to maintain the current "connected component" of positions that can contain the 1

Since we start with just position x, we can think of it as maintaining a connected interval that contains x initially, and we expand it whenever we find an operation whose range overlaps with our current interval.

The process:
- Start with current_interval = [x, x] (a single point)
- For each operation [l_i, r_i]:
  - If current_interval overlaps with [l_i, r_i], then we can merge them
  - The new interval becomes the merge of current_interval and [l_i, r_i]
- The final answer is the size of the final interval

## Step-by-step execution with examples

**Example 1:** n=6, x=4, operations: [1,6], [2,3], [5,5]
- Start: interval = [4,4]
- Operation [1,6]: [1,6] overlaps with [4,4]? Yes → new interval = [1,6]
- Operation [2,3]: [2,3] overlaps with [1,6]? Yes → new interval = [1,6]
- Operation [5,5]: overlaps → still [1,6]
- Answer: 6-1+1 = 6 ✓

**Example 2:** n=4, x=1, operations: [2,4], [1,2]
- Start: interval = [1,1]
- Operation [2,4]: [2,4] overlaps with [1,1]? No (1 is not in [2,4]) → interval remains [1,1]
- Operation [1,2]: [1,2] overlaps with [1,1]? Yes → new interval = [1,2]
- Answer: 2-1+1 = 2 ✓

**Example 3:** n=3, x=3, operations: [2,3], [1,2]
- Start: interval = [3,3]
- Operation [2,3]: [2,3] overlaps with [3,3]? Yes → new interval = [2,3]
- Operation [1,2]: [1,2] overlaps with [2,3]? Yes → new interval = [1,3]
- Answer: 3-1+1 = 3 ✓

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n, x, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Start with the initial position x
    left = x
    right = x
    
    for _ in range(m):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        
        # Check if current interval [left, right] overlaps with [l, r]
        # Two intervals overlap if they have any common point
        # That happens when left <= r and l <= right
        if left <= r and l <= right:
            # They overlap, so we can merge them
            # The new interval is from min(left, l) to max(right, r)
            left = min(left, l)
            right = max(right, r)
    
    # The number of indices in the interval [left, right] is right - left + 1
    return right - left + 1

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    print(solve())
```

The algorithm works because:
- Initially, only position x can have the 1
- Each operation allows us to swap any two elements in its range [l_i, r_i]
- If our current reachable range overlaps with an operation's range, we can expand our reachable range to include the entire operation range
- This is because we can use the operation to move the 1 from our current position to any position within the operation's range
- The final interval represents all positions from which we can reach the initial position x through a series of operations

Time complexity: O(m) per test case, which is efficient given that m ≤ 100.
Space complexity: O(1).

def solve():
    n, x, m = map(int, input().split())
    
    # Start with the initial position x
    left = x
    right = x
    
    for _ in range(m):
        l, r = map(int, input().split())
        
        # Check if current interval [left, right] overlaps with [l, r]
        # Two intervals overlap if they have any common point
        # That happens when left <= r and l <= right
        if left <= r and l <= right:
            # They overlap, so we can merge them
            # The new interval is from min(left, l) to max(right, r)
            left = min(left, l)
            right = max(right, r)
    
    # The number of indices in the interval [left, right] is right - left + 1
    return right - left + 1

t = int(input())

    print(solve())

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Once Vasya played bricks. All the bricks in the set had regular cubical shape. Vasya vas a talented architect, however the tower he built kept falling apart.

Let us consider the building process. Vasya takes a brick and puts it on top of the already built tower so that the sides of the brick are parallel to the sides of the bricks he has already used. Let's introduce a Cartesian coordinate system on the horizontal plane, where Vasya puts the first brick. Then the projection of brick number i on the plane is a square with sides parallel to the axes of coordinates with opposite corners in points (xi, 1, yi, 1) and (xi, 2, yi, 2). The bricks are cast from homogeneous plastic and the weight of a brick a × a × a is a3 grams.

It is guaranteed that Vasya puts any brick except the first one on the previous one, that is the area of intersection of the upper side of the previous brick and the lower side of the next brick is always positive.

We (Vasya included) live in a normal world where the laws of physical statics work. And that is why, perhaps, if we put yet another brick, the tower will collapse under its own weight. Vasya puts the cubes consecutively one on top of the other until at least one cube loses the balance and falls down. If it happens, Vasya gets upset and stops the construction. Print the number of bricks in the maximal stable tower, that is the maximal number m satisfying the condition that all the towers consisting of bricks 1, 2, ..., k for every integer k from 1 to m remain stable.

Input

The first input file contains an integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100) which is the number of bricks. Each of the next n lines contains four numbers xi, 1, yi, 1, xi, 2, yi, 2 (xi, 1 ≠ xi, 2, |xi, 1 - xi, 2| = |yi, 1 - yi, 2|) which are the coordinates of the opposite angles of the base of the brick number i. The coordinates are integers and their absolute value does not exceed 50. 

The cubes are given in the order Vasya puts them. It is guaranteed that the area of intersection of the upper side of the brick number i - 1 and the lower side of the brick number i is strictly strictly greater than zero for all i ≥ 2.

Output

Print the number of bricks in the maximal stable tower.

Examples

Input

2
0 0 3 3
1 0 4 3


Output

2

2
0 0 3 3
2 0 5 3


Output

1

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tower of bricks (cubes) placed one on top of another
2. Each brick has a square base with sides parallel to axes
3. The weight of a brick with side length `a` is `a³` grams
4. The tower collapses when at least one brick loses balance
5. We need to find the maximum number of bricks in a stable tower

## Key Physics Concept

For a tower to be stable, every brick must be balanced. The key insight is that each brick must be supported by the brick below it, and the center of mass of the entire structure above a certain point must lie within the support area.

However, there's a crucial simplification: since we're dealing with a sequence of bricks where each new brick sits on top of the previous one, and we need to check stability at each step, we need to consider the center of mass.

## Stability Condition

For a tower to be stable when adding a new brick, the center of mass of all bricks above any given point must lie within the support area of that point. Since we're dealing with a stack, we need to check if the center of mass of the entire stack of bricks from the current position downward lies within the base of each brick.

Wait, I think I found the issue. Let me look at the second example again:

Brick 1: from (0,0) to (3,3) - center at (1.5, 1.5)
Brick 2: from (2,0) to (5,3) - center at (3.5, 1.5)

When brick 2 is placed on brick 1, the center of mass of the entire system is at (2.5, 1.5). 

For brick 1 to be stable, the center of mass of the bricks above it must be within its base. The bricks above brick 1 are only brick 2. The center of mass of brick 2 is at (3.5, 1.5). Is (3.5, 1.5) within brick 1's base (0,0) to (3,3)? No, because 3.5 > 3.

Ah! That's it! The center of mass of the brick above (brick 2) must lie within the base of the brick below (brick 1).

Similarly, for the top brick (brick 2), there are no bricks above it, so it's stable by itself.

So the stability condition is: when we add brick k on top of brick k-1, the center of mass of all bricks above brick i (for each i) must lie within the base of brick i.

In this case, when we add brick 2, we need to check for brick 1: the center of mass of bricks above it (only brick 2) is at (3.5, 1.5), which is outside brick 1's base (0,0) to (3,3), so it's unstable.

In the first example:
- Brick 1: (0,0) to (3,3) - center (1.5, 1.5)
- Brick 2: (1,0) to (4,3) - center (2.5, 1.5)

- For brick 2: no bricks above, so stable.
- So tower of 2 bricks is stable.

For the third example with 3 bricks:
- Brick 1: (0,0) to (3,3) - center (1.5, 1.5)
- Brick 2: (1,0) to (4,3) - center (2.5, 1.5)
- Brick 3: (2,0) to (5,3) - center (3.5, 1.5)

Adding brick 3 on top of brick 2:
- For brick 1: bricks above it are bricks 2 and 3. Center of mass of bricks 2 and 3: 
  - Weight of each brick: 3³ = 27
  - Center of mass: ((2.5 + 3.5)/2, (1.5 + 1.5)/2) = (3, 1.5)
  - Is (3, 1.5) within brick 1's base (0,0) to (3,3)? Yes, since 0 ≤ 3 ≤ 3 and 0 ≤ 1.5 ≤ 3.
- For brick 2: bricks above it is brick 3. Center of mass: (3.5, 1.5). Is it within brick 2's base (1,0) to (4,3)? Yes, since 1 ≤ 3.5 ≤ 4 and 0 ≤ 1.5 ≤ 3.
- For brick 3: no bricks above, stable.
- So tower of 3 bricks is stable.

But the output is 3, which matches.

Therefore, the algorithm is:

1. Read the input bricks
2. For each possible tower size from 1 to n, check if it's stable
3. A tower of k bricks is stable if for each brick i (from 1 to k), the center of mass of all bricks j where j > i lies within the base of brick i
4. If a tower is stable, it can grow; otherwise, it stops
5. Return the maximum k for which the tower is stable

Actually, we need to check stability when we add each brick sequentially. We start with 1 brick (always stable). Then for each subsequent brick k, we check if adding it makes the entire tower of k bricks stable.

To check if a tower of k bricks is stable, for each brick i in the tower (from 1 to k), we compute the center of mass of all bricks with index > i (the bricks strictly above brick i), and check if this center of mass lies within the base of brick i.

Note: the bricks are given in order, so brick 1 is first, brick 2 is second, etc.

The center of mass of a set of bricks with known centers and equal weights (since each brick has the same shape and density) is the average of the centers.

But wait - each brick has side length a, so weight is a³. The side length is |x1 - x2| or |y1 - y2|, since it's a square.

So we need to calculate the actual weight (volume) of each brick.

Algorithm:

1. Read n bricks
2. For each brick, compute its side length and center coordinates
3. Initialize a variable `max_stable` = 0
4. For k from 1 to n:
   - Create a list of the first k bricks
   - Check if this tower of k bricks is stable
   - To check stability, for each brick i (0-indexed) in the first k bricks:
     - Find all bricks j where j > i (bricks above brick i)
     - Compute the center of mass of those bricks (weighted by their volume)
     - Check if this center of mass lies within the base of brick i
   - If the tower of k bricks is stable, set max_stable = k
   - Else, break (since adding more bricks will make it more unstable)
5. Print max_stable

But is it possible that a tower of k bricks is unstable, but a tower of k+1 bricks is stable? I don't think so - adding more bricks on top increases the weight on the lower bricks, making them more likely to tip. So if adding brick k+1 makes the tower of k+1 bricks unstable, then k+1 is not valid, and the stability should break at k+1.

Actually, it's possible that the center of mass shifts in a way that makes the tower more stable, but in this case, since we're adding a brick on top (which increases the weight above the lower bricks), it would generally make the system less stable. So the stability should be non-increasing as we add bricks.

Therefore, we can simulate from k=1 to n, and stop when we find an instability.

Let me implement this.

```python
n = int(input())
bricks = []
for _ in range(n):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
    # Ensure x1 <= x2 and y1 <= y2
    if x1 > x2:
        x1, x2 = x2, x1
    if y1 > y2:
        y1, y2 = y2, y1
    side = x2 - x1
    center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
    center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
    volume = side ** 3
    bricks.append((center_x, center_y, volume, x1, y1, x2, y2))

def is_stable(tower):
    # tower is a list of indices from 0 to k-1
    # for each brick i in the tower, check if center of mass of bricks above it lies within its base
    k = len(tower)
    for i in range(k):
        # Find all bricks above brick i (j > i)
        above_centers = []
        above_volumes = []
        for j in range(i + 1, k):
            center_x, center_y, vol, x1, y1, x2, y2 = bricks[tower[j]]
            above_centers.append((center_x, center_y))
            above_volumes.append(vol)
        
        # If there are no bricks above, it's stable by default
        if not above_centers:
            continue
            
        # Compute center of mass of bricks above
        total_volume = sum(above_volumes)
        if total_volume == 0:
            # This shouldn't happen if we have bricks above
            center_x_cm = 0
            center_y_cm = 0
        else:
            total_x = sum(center_x * vol for center_x, center_y in above_centers)
            total_y = sum(center_y * vol for center_x, center_y in above_centers)
            center_x_cm = total_x / total_volume
            center_y_cm = total_y / total_volume
        
        # Get the base of brick i
        x1_i, y1_i, x2_i, y2_i = bricks[tower[i]][3], bricks[tower[i]][4], bricks[tower[i]][5], bricks[tower[i]][6]
        
        # Check if center of mass lies within the base
        if not (x1_i <= center_x_cm <= x2_i and y1_i <= center_y_cm <= y2_i):
            return False
    return True

# Check from 1 brick up to n bricks
max_stable = 0
for k in range(1, n + 1):
    tower_indices = list(range(k))
    if is_stable(tower_indices):
        max_stable = k
    else:
        break

print(max_stable)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Once Vasya played bricks. All the bricks in the set had regular cubical shape. Vasya vas a talented architect, however the tower he built kept falling apart.

Let us consider the building process. Vasya takes a brick and puts it on top of the already built tower so that the sides of the brick are parallel to the sides of the bricks he has already used. Let's introduce a Cartesian coordinate system on the horizontal plane, where Vasya puts the first brick. Then the projection of brick number i on the plane is a square with sides parallel to the axes of coordinates with opposite corners in points (xi, 1, yi, 1) and (xi, 2, yi, 2). The bricks are cast from homogeneous plastic and the weight of a brick a × a × a is a3 grams.

It is guaranteed that Vasya puts any brick except the first one on the previous one, that is the area of intersection of the upper side of the previous brick and the lower side of the next brick is always positive.

We (Vasya included) live in a normal world where the laws of physical statics work. And that is why, perhaps, if we put yet another brick, the tower will collapse under its own weight. Vasya puts the cubes consecutively one on top of the other until at least one cube loses the balance and falls down. If it happens, Vasya gets upset and stops the construction. Print the number of bricks in the maximal stable tower, that is the maximal number m satisfying the condition that all the towers consisting of bricks 1, 2, ..., k for every integer k from 1 to m remain stable.

Input

The first input file contains an integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100) which is the number of bricks. Each of the next n lines contains four numbers xi, 1, yi, 1, xi, 2, yi, 2 (xi, 1 ≠ xi, 2, |xi, 1 - xi, 2| = |yi, 1 - yi, 2|) which are the coordinates of the opposite angles of the base of the brick number i. The coordinates are integers and their absolute value does not exceed 50. 

The cubes are given in the order Vasya puts them. It is guaranteed that the area of intersection of the upper side of the brick number i - 1 and the lower side of the brick number i is strictly strictly greater than zero for all i ≥ 2.

Output

Print the number of bricks in the maximal stable tower.

Examples

Input

2
0 0 3 3
1 0 4 3


Output

2


Input

2
0 0 3 3
2 0 5 3


Output

1

3
0 0 3 3
1 0 4 3
2 0 5 3

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have bricks that are cubes with sides parallel to axes
2. Bricks are placed one on top of another in a specific order
3. Each brick has a base that's a square (since it's a cube)
4. The tower collapses when any brick loses balance
5. We need to find the maximum number of bricks that can be placed such that the tower remains stable

## Key Insight

For a tower to be stable, each brick must be able to support the weight of all bricks above it. However, since the bricks are cubes and placed on top of each other, the key physical principle is that each brick must be supported by the one below it.

The crucial point is that for a brick to be stable, its center of mass must be above the support area of the brick below it. Since we're dealing with cubes, the center of mass of each cube is at its geometric center.

For a stable tower, each brick (except the bottom one) must have its center of mass within the projection of the brick below it.

## Step-by-step Solution

1. Parse the input to get the coordinates of each brick's base
2. For each brick, calculate its center of mass (geometric center of the square base)
3. Check stability starting from the bottom brick and moving upward
4. For each brick (from bottom to top), verify that its center of mass lies within the projection of the brick below it
5. If at any point a brick's center of mass is outside the support area of the brick below it, the tower becomes unstable
6. Find the maximum number of bricks that can be placed before instability occurs

## Algorithm

1. For each brick i, extract its coordinates and calculate its center
2. Start with the first brick (bottom) - it's always stable
3. For each subsequent brick, check if its center lies within the base of the brick below it
4. If it doesn't, then the tower is unstable at that point
5. The answer is the number of bricks up to and including the last stable brick

## Implementation Details

For a brick with corners (x1, y1) and (x2, y2), the center is at ((x1+x2)/2, (y1+y2)/2)

For a brick to be stable on another, the center of the upper brick must lie within the rectangular area of the lower brick.

Note: Since the bricks are squares and the sides are parallel, we need to check if the center of the upper brick falls within the bounds of the lower brick.

```python
n = int(input())
bricks = []
for _ in range(n):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
    # Normalize coordinates so x1 <= x2 and y1 <= y2
    x1, x2 = min(x1, x2), max(x1, x2)
    y1, y2 = min(y1, y2), max(y1, y2)
    bricks.append((x1, y1, x2, y2))

# For each brick, calculate the center of mass
centers = []
for x1, y1, x2, y2 in bricks:
    cx = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
    cy = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
    centers.append((cx, cy))

# Check stability starting from the first brick
# We need to check if each brick (from 2nd to last) is stable on the previous one
max_stable = 1  # At least the first brick is stable

# For a brick k (k >= 1) to be stable on brick k-1 (0-indexed), 
# the center of brick k must be within the base of brick k-1
for k in range(1, n):
    # Check if centers[k] is within the bounds of brick k-1
    x_center, y_center = centers[k]
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = bricks[k-1]
    
    # The center must be within the x and y bounds of the lower brick
    if not (x1 <= x_center <= x2 and y1 <= y_center <= y2):
        # The tower becomes unstable at this point
        max_stable = k
        break
    else:
        # The tower is stable up to this point
        max_stable = k + 1

print(max_stable)
```

- So max_stable = 2 ✓

- Brick 1 has x range [0,3], so 0 ≤ 2.5 ≤ 3 → True, 0 ≤ 1.5 ≤ 3 → True → stable
- Brick 3: (2,0) to (5,3), center = (3.5, 1.5)
- Check if (3.5, 1.5) is in brick 2's base (1,0) to (4,3): 1 ≤ 3.5 ≤ 4 → True, 0 ≤ 1.5 ≤ 3 → True → stable
- So max_stable = 3 ✓

```python
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
    # Normalize coordinates so x1 <= x2 and y1 <= y2
    x1, x2 = min(x1, x2), max(x1, x2)
    y1, y2 = min(y1, y2), max(y1, y2)
    bricks.append((x1, y1, x2, y2))

# For each brick, calculate the center of mass
centers = []
for x1, y1, x2, y2 in bricks:
    cx = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
    cy = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
    centers.append((cx, cy))

# Check stability starting from the first brick
# We need to check if each brick (from 2nd to last) is stable on the previous one
max_stable = 1  # At least the first brick is stable

# For a brick k (k >= 1) to be stable on brick k-1 (0-indexed), 
# the center of brick k must be within the base of brick k-1
for k in range(1, n):
    # Check if centers[k] is within the bounds of brick k-1
    x_center, y_center = centers[k]
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = bricks[k-1]
    
    # The center must be within the x and y bounds of the lower brick
    if not (x1 <= x_center <= x2 and y1 <= y_center <= y2):
        # The tower becomes unstable at this point
        max_stable = k
        break
    else:
        # The tower is stable up to this point
        max_stable = k + 1

print(max_stable)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Once Vasya played bricks. All the bricks in the set had regular cubical shape. Vasya vas a talented architect, however the tower he built kept falling apart.

Let us consider the building process. Vasya takes a brick and puts it on top of the already built tower so that the sides of the brick are parallel to the sides of the bricks he has already used. Let's introduce a Cartesian coordinate system on the horizontal plane, where Vasya puts the first brick. Then the projection of brick number i on the plane is a square with sides parallel to the axes of coordinates with opposite corners in points (xi, 1, yi, 1) and (xi, 2, yi, 2). The bricks are cast from homogeneous plastic and the weight of a brick a × a × a is a3 grams.

It is guaranteed that Vasya puts any brick except the first one on the previous one, that is the area of intersection of the upper side of the previous brick and the lower side of the next brick is always positive.

We (Vasya included) live in a normal world where the laws of physical statics work. And that is why, perhaps, if we put yet another brick, the tower will collapse under its own weight. Vasya puts the cubes consecutively one on top of the other until at least one cube loses the balance and falls down. If it happens, Vasya gets upset and stops the construction. Print the number of bricks in the maximal stable tower, that is the maximal number m satisfying the condition that all the towers consisting of bricks 1, 2, ..., k for every integer k from 1 to m remain stable.

Input

The first input file contains an integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 100) which is the number of bricks. Each of the next n lines contains four numbers xi, 1, yi, 1, xi, 2, yi, 2 (xi, 1 ≠ xi, 2, |xi, 1 - xi, 2| = |yi, 1 - yi, 2|) which are the coordinates of the opposite angles of the base of the brick number i. The coordinates are integers and their absolute value does not exceed 50. 

The cubes are given in the order Vasya puts them. It is guaranteed that the area of intersection of the upper side of the brick number i - 1 and the lower side of the brick number i is strictly strictly greater than zero for all i ≥ 2.

Output

Print the number of bricks in the maximal stable tower.

Examples

Input

2
0 0 3 3
1 0 4 3


Output

2


Input

2
0 0 3 3
2 0 5 3


Output

1


Input

3
0 0 3 3
1 0 4 3
2 0 5 3

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have bricks placed one on top of another in a specific order
2. Each brick is a cube with sides parallel to axes
3. The weight of a brick is a³ grams where a is the side length
4. The tower collapses when at least one brick loses balance
5. We need to find the maximum number of bricks such that all partial towers remain stable

## Key Insight

The stability of the tower depends on the center of mass. For each brick, its center of mass must be supported by the brick below it. 

For a stable tower, the center of mass of each brick (including the brick itself and all bricks above it) must be within the base area of the brick below it.

Let me think about this more carefully:

- When we add a new brick on top, we need to check if it will cause any of the existing bricks to lose stability
- Actually, the problem is asking for the maximum number of bricks such that ALL partial towers (1 brick, 2 bricks, ..., k bricks) remain stable
- So we need to check each prefix of the sequence - for each k, check if the tower of height k is stable

## Stability Condition

For a tower to be stable, the center of mass of every brick must lie within the projection of the brick below it.

The center of mass of the entire structure up to brick k should be such that each individual brick's center of mass is supported by the brick below it.

Actually, let's think about it systematically:

For a brick placed at position i, when we have a tower of height k (with k ≥ i), the center of mass of the entire structure from brick 1 to brick k must be positioned such that the center of mass of bricks 1 to i lies within the projection of brick i-1 (for i ≥ 2), and so on.

But actually, the problem is simpler: when we place a brick on top of the previous one, the stability depends on the center of mass of the current brick and the bricks above it.

Wait, let me re-read: "if we put yet another brick, the tower will collapse under its own weight. Vasya puts the cubes consecutively one on top of the other until at least one cube loses the balance and falls down."

This suggests we need to check each step. But the output is the maximum m such that towers of size 1,2,...,m are all stable.

So we need to:
1. For each prefix of the sequence (1 brick, 2 bricks, ..., k bricks), check if that tower is stable
2. Find the maximum k where all prefixes are stable
3. Return that k

## How to check stability?

For each brick i, we need to ensure that its center of mass (with respect to the bricks above/below it) stays within the base of the brick below it.

Actually, for a stable tower, each brick must support the total weight above it.

For brick i, when considering stability of the brick at position i, it must support all the bricks above it (i+1, i+2, ..., k) in terms of center of mass.

The key insight is that for a tower to be stable at the k-th level, each brick j (where j ≤ k) must have its center of mass (of the entire structure from 1 to k) lie within the projection of the brick below it.

But actually, for brick j to support the bricks above it, the center of mass of bricks j+1 to k must lie within the projection of brick j.

Let me think differently:

When adding brick k, we need to check if the center of mass of bricks k and k-1 and so on is supported properly.

Actually, each brick i supports the weight of all bricks above it (i+1 to k). The center of mass of the total weight above brick i must lie within the projection of brick i.

So for each brick i from 1 to k-1, the center of mass of bricks i+1 to k must lie within the projection of brick i.

For brick k (the top one), it only needs to be supported by brick k-1, and so on.

Wait, no. When we have a sequence of bricks, for the system to be stable:

- The center of mass of all bricks from i+1 to k must lie within the projection of brick i, for every i from 1 to k-1.

This is because brick i bears the weight of all bricks above it.

## Algorithm

1. Read the input
2. For each k from 1 to n (in order), check if the tower of height k is stable
3. For the tower of height k, check that for each brick i from 1 to k-1, the center of mass of bricks i+1 to k lies within the projection of brick i
4. The center of mass is computed as the weighted average of the centers of mass of each brick (weighted by their volume, which is side^3)
5. Early stop: if any tower of height k is unstable, then all towers of height greater than k are also unstable, so we return k-1
6. If all towers up to k are stable, continue

## Implementation Steps

1. Parse input to get coordinates of each brick
2. For each brick, compute its center and side length
3. For each k from 1 to n:
   - For each i from 1 to k-1:
     - Compute center of mass of bricks i+1 to k (weighted by their volumes)
     - Check if this center lies within the projection of brick i
   - If any i fails, return k-1
4. If all check, return k

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    bricks = []
    
    for _ in range(n):
        x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
        # Normalize coordinates so x1 <= x2, y1 <= y2
        if x1 > x2:
            x1, x2 = x2, x1
        if y1 > y2:
            y1, y2 = y2, y1
        side = abs(x2 - x1)  # or abs(y2 - y1), they should be equal
        center_x = (x1 + x2) / 2.0
        center_y = (y1 + y2) / 2.0
        bricks.append((center_x, center_y, side))
    
    # Check stability for each tower size k from 1 to n
    for k in range(1, n + 1):
        stable = True
        # Check each brick i from 1 to k-1
        for i in range(k - 1):  # i is 0-indexed, represents brick i (0-indexed)
            # We need to check if brick i can support bricks i+1 to k-1 (0-indexed)
            # i+1 to k-1 are bricks from index i+1 to k-1
            if i + 1 > k - 1:  # no bricks above
                continue
                
            # Calculate center of mass of bricks from i+1 to k-1
            total_mass = 0.0
            total_mass_x = 0.0
            total_mass_y = 0.0
            
            for j in range(i + 1, k):
                side_j = bricks[j][2]
                mass_j = side_j ** 3
                center_x_j, center_y_j, _ = bricks[j]
                total_mass += mass_j
                total_mass_x += mass_j * center_x_j
                total_mass_y += mass_j * center_y_j
            
            if total_mass == 0:
                continue
                
            center_of_mass_x = total_mass_x / total_mass
            center_of_mass_y = total_mass_y / total_mass
            
            # Get the projection of brick i
            center_x_i, center_y_i, side_i = bricks[i]
            half_side = side_i / 2.0
            left_x = center_x_i - half_side
            right_x = center_x_i + half_side
            bottom_y = center_y_i - half_side
            top_y = center_y_i + half_side
            
            # Check if the center of mass lies within this projection
            if not (left_x <= center_of_mass_x <= right_x and bottom_y <= center_of_mass_y <= top_y):
                stable = False
                break
        
        if not stable:
            print(k - 1)
            return
    
    print(n)

solve()
```

```python
n = int(input())
bricks = []

for _ in range(n):
    x1, y1, x2, y2 = map(int, input().split())
    # Normalize coordinates
    if x1 > x2:
        x1, x2 = x2, x1
    if y1 > y2:

    # For each brick i (0-indexed) from 0 to k-2, check if it supports bricks i+1 to k-1

            total_mass_x += mass_j * center_x_j
            total_mass_y += mass_j * center_y_j
        
        if total_mass == 0:
            continue

        center_of_mass_y = total_mass_y / total_mass

        right_x = center_x_i + half_side
        bottom_y = center_y_i - half_side
        top_y = center_y_i + half_side
```
